The full range of uniform bounds for the bilinear Hilbert transform
Gennady Uraltsev, Michał Warchalski

Abstract
. We prove uniform L p L^{p} bounds for the family of bilinear Hilbert transforms BHT β ​ [ f 1 , f 2 ] ​ ( x ) := p . v . ∫ ℝ f 1 ​ ( x − t ) ​ f 2 ​ ( x + β ​ t ) ​ d ​ t t \mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}[f_{1},f_{2}](x):=\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{1}(x-t)f_{2}(x+\beta t)\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t} . We show that the operator BHT β \mathrm{BHT}_{\beta} maps L p 1 × L p 2 L^{p_{1}}\times L^{p_{2}} into L p L^{p} as long as p 1 ∈ ( 1 , ∞ ) p_{1}\in(1,\infty) , p 2 ∈ ( 1 , ∞ ) p_{2}\in(1,\infty) , and p > 2 3 p>\frac{2}{3} with a bound independent of β ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] \beta\in(0,1] . This is the full open range of exponents where the modulation invariant class of bilinear operators containing BHT β \mathrm{BHT}_{\beta} can be bounded uniformly. This is done by proving boundedness of certain affine transformations of the frequency-time-scale space ℝ + 3 \mathbb{R}^{3}_{+} in terms of iterated outer Lebesgue spaces. This results in new linear and bilinear wave packet embedding bounds well suited to study uniform bounds.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 42B20, 42B35, Secondary 46B70, 47A07, 47B38

1.
Introduction

The bilinear Hilbert transform with parameter
\beta\in(0,1]
is a bilinear
singular integral operator given by

(1.1)

\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}[f_{1},f_{2}](x)\coloneqq\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{1}(x-t)f_{2}(x+\beta t)\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

where
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
are Schwartz functions on the real
line. It arises in diverse context: averages of (
1.1
) over
\beta

can yield relevant correction terms for evaluating Cauchy integrals along
Lipschitz domains in
\mathbb{C}
and for inverting elliptic operators with
non-constant coefficients; fiberwise applications of bounds on (
1.1
)
can be used to obtain bounds on multilinear singular integral operators with
full
\operatorname{Gl}_{2}
symmetry. We elaborate and provide references for these
applications further on.

In this paper we are interested in showing the a-priori bounds

(1.2)

\|\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}[f_{1},f_{2}]\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C_{p_{1},p_{2},\beta}\|f_{1}\|_{L^{p_{1}}(\mathbb{R})}\|f_{2}\|_{L^{p_{2}}(\mathbb{R})},\qquad p^{-1}=p_{1}^{-1}+p_{2}^{-1}

for some constant
C_{p_{1},p_{2},\beta}>0
that does not depend on
f_{1}
and

f_{2}
. A scaling argument shows that the constraints on the exponents
(p_{1},p_{2},p)
in (
1.2
) must hold. In

[
LT97
]
, Lacey and Thiele proved the first
estimates of the type (
1.2
): they showed that the bound holds
in the range
2<p_{1},p_{2}<\infty
,
p<2
. This corresponds to the open
triangle
c
in
Figure

1
. The range of exponents for
(
1.2
) was later extended by Lacey and Thiele in

[
LT98
]
to the convex hull of the open
triangles
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
in
Figure

1
, where bounds below the line

(0,1,0)-(0,0,1)
and to the upper right side of the line
(0,1,0)-(0,0,1)
are to be intended in the restricted weak sense, discussed in

[
Thi06
]
. Inspired by the works of Carleson

[
Car66
]
and Fefferman

[
Fef73
]
the authors of the above
results used the paradigm of time scale frequency analysis.

When
\beta=0
the operator
\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}[f_{1},f_{2}](x)
becomes simpler
and is given by the pointwise product of the Hilbert transform of
f_{1}
with

f_{2}
:

(1.3)

\mathrm{BHT}_{0}[f_{1},f_{2}](x)=\mathrm{H}f_{1}(x)\,f_{2}(x);

this immediately implies boundedness for any
p_{1},p_{2}\in(1,\infty)
by the
boundedness of
f\mapsto\mathrm{H}f
on
L^{p_{1}}(\mathbb{R})
for any
p_{1}\in(1,\infty)
and by Hölder’s inequality. For any fixed
p_{1},p_{2}\in(1,\infty)
, the constant
C_{p_{1},p_{2},\beta}
obtained in

[
LT97
]
and

[
LT98
]
, while this is not stated
explicitly, grows linearly in
|\beta|^{-1}
. Naturally, in

[
LT98
]
the authors posed the question of
whether bounds (
1.2
) can be shown with a constant
C_{p_{1},p_{2}}
independent of
\beta\in(0,1]
, in place of
C_{p_{1},p_{2},\beta}
,
and if so, for what range of exponents
p_{1},p_{2}
. This is in accordance with
the observation that in the limit
\beta=0
we are still dealing with a
bounded operator. These bounds are referred to as “uniform bounds”; any
bound that holds only for
\beta\gtrsim 1
or with a constant that is allowed
to depend on
\beta
, will be referred to in this paper as a “non-uniform”
bound.

There is a rich collection of partial results concerning uniform variants of
(
1.2
). In
[
Thi02
]
Thiele
established weak uniform bounds at the two upper corners of the triangle
c

in
Figure

1
. Grafakos and Li in

[
GL04
]
showed the uniform inequality in
the open triangle
c
, and Li
[
Li06
]
proved the
uniform bounds in the open triangles
a_{1}

a_{2}
. By interpolation one obtains
the uniform variant of (
1.2
) in the range corresponding to the
convex hull of the open triangles
a_{2}
,
a_{3}
, and
c
. What, however, was
not known up to date, is whether the uniform bounds hold in the vicinity of

(p_{1},p_{2})=(\infty,1)
and
(p_{1},p_{2})=(\infty,\infty)
i.e. in the
corners of triangles
b_{2}
and
b_{3}
. This is precisely the main result of
this work.

Theorem 1
.

Bounds (
1.2
) holds for all functions
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
as long as
p_{1}\in(1,\infty)
,
p_{2}\in(1,\infty)
, and
p>\frac{2}{3}
with a constant dependent only on
p_{1}

and
p_{2}
but not on
\beta\in(0,1]
.

Our methods cover directly the regions
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3},c
in

Figure

1
. The extension to the full shaded region in

Figure

1
can be obtained by interpolation with the result in

[
Li06
]
. As noted before, bounds on the same
side of the triangle
a_{3}
of the segment
(0,1,0)-(0,0,1)
are to be
intended in the restricted weak type sense. Otherwise, we believe our methods
can be used to obtain a uniform sparse domination of the trilinear form
associated to
\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}
that would imply
Theorem

1
in the full
range. However, we leave this for possible subsequent works.

Since the Hilbert transform is not bounded on
L^{\infty}
, bounds
(
1.2
) cannot hold uniformly below the
(0,1,0)-(0,0,1)

segment. Thus,
Theorem

1
covers the full range where bounds
(
1.2
) can hold uniformly in
\beta\in(0,1]
, inside the
range where bounds (
1.2
) are known to hold at all
(non-uniformly). Furthermore Lacey in

[
Lac00
]
shows that the above range of
exponents is sharp, modulo endpoints, for the non-uniform modulation invariant
class of bilinear operators.

Oberlin and Thiele, showed
Theorem

1
for a Walsh model of the bilinear
Hilbert transform. The techniques there rely crucially on the perfect time
frequency analysis of the Walsh phase space and on the possibility of
constructing phase space tree projections, tools that we were unable to
generalize for obtaining the current result in the real case.

Figure 1.

Points
(p_{1}^{-1},p_{2}^{-1},1-p^{-1})\in(-1,1)^{3}
with
p^{-1}=p_{1}^{-1}+p_{2}^{-1}
for which (
1.2
) may hold, represented
as affine combinations.

1.1.
Applications

The bilinear Hilbert transform has shown up in multiple circumstances. One of
the original motivations for the interest in
\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}
was Calderón’s
first commutator
C_{1}(f,A)
, that arises naturally as a correction term when
studying solutions for elliptic operators with non-smooth coefficients. In the
one-dimensional case one has

(1.4)

C_{1}(f,A)(x)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{A(x)-A(y)}{(x-y)^{2}}f(y)\mathrm{d}y.

In
[
Cal65
]
Calderón showed that
f\mapsto C_{1}(f,A)
is bounded on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
for all
p\in(1,\infty)
when
\|A\|_{\operatorname{Lip}}<+\infty
. Formally rewriting
C_{1}(f,A)
using the fundamental theorem of calculus yields

(1.5)

C_{1}(f,A)(x)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)A^{\prime}(y+\beta(x-y))\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{x-y}\mathrm{d}\beta=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}[f,A^{\prime}]\mathrm{d}\beta.

Dualizing and enacting a change of variables allows one to recover Calderón’s
result from
[
Li06
]
. An alternative proof of the
boundedness of Calderón’s first commutator was given in

[
Mus14
]
. For a more detailed discussion
of Calderón’s commutators see Chapter 4 of

[
MS13
]
.

More recently in
[
Gre+16
]
, bounds on a
trilinear form with full
\operatorname{GL}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2}\right)
symmetry acting on
functions on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
was obtained by a fiber-wise application of uniform bounds
in the range covered by
[
GL04
]
.

Generalizations of the bilinear Hilbert transform to functions on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
have
been systematically studied in

[
DT10
]
. Instead of just one
parameter
\beta
, the corresponding trilinear form on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is determined by
three matrixes
\vec{B}=(B_{j})_{j\in\{1,2,3\}}\in\left(\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}\right)^{3}
and is given by

(1.6)

\operatorname{BHF}^{K}_{\vec{B}}(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{3}f_{j}\left(\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\end{pmatrix}+B_{j}\begin{pmatrix}s\\
t\end{pmatrix}\right)K(s,t)\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y,

where
K
is a two-dimensional smooth Calderón-Zygmund kernel. If
B
is
non-invertible, one can obtain completely novel multilinear integral
operators, some of which have bee n studied independently (see Kovač’s work on
the twisted paraproduct in
[
Kov12
]
),
while others remain beyond the current level understanding, like the
triangular Hilbert transform (see
[
KTZK15
]
).
A partial investigation of uniform bounds in the context of the 2-dimensional
bilinear Hilbert transform has been carried by the second author in his PhD
thesis
[
War19
]
. A preliminary version of
this joint work also appeared there.

1.2.
Wave packet representation and the space of symmetries

The bilinear Hilbert transform is symmetric under translations, modulations,
and dilations, given by

(1.7)

\begin{aligned} &\mathrm{Tr}_{y}\varphi(z)\coloneqq\varphi(z-y),\\
&\mathrm{Mod}_{\eta}\varphi(z)\coloneqq e^{2\pi i\eta z}\varphi(z),\\
&\mathrm{Dil}_{t}\varphi(z)\coloneqq t^{-1}\varphi\left(\frac{z}{t}\right),\end{aligned}\quad(\eta,y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},

that act on the functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
. In particular, up to a linear
combination with the pointwise product,
\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}
is the unique bilinear
operator that satisfies

(1.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}\left[\mathrm{Tr}_{y}f_{1},\mathrm{Tr}_{y}f_{2}\right]=\mathrm{Tr}_{y}\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}[f_{1},f_{2}]\qquad\forall y\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}\left[\mathrm{Mod}_{\beta\eta}f_{1},\mathrm{Mod}_{\eta}f_{2}\right]=\mathrm{Mod}_{(1+\beta)\eta}\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}[f_{1},f_{2}]\qquad\forall\eta\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle t^{2}\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}\left[\mathrm{Dil}_{t}f_{1},\mathrm{Dil}_{t}f_{2}\right]=t\mathrm{Dil}_{t}\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}[f_{1},f_{2}]\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}.

Based on this we show that
\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}
admits the following wave packet
representation

(1.9)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi i}\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}[f_{1},f_{2}](x)=f_{1}(x)f_{2}(x)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &-C_{\beta}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{1}](\eta,y,t)[\varphi_{0}]\mathrm{Emb}[f_{2}](\beta^{-1}\eta-(\beta t)^{-1},y,\beta t)[\varphi_{0}]\\
&\qquad\times\mathrm{Tr}_{y}\mathrm{Mod}_{\frac{1+\beta}{\beta}\eta-(\beta t)^{-1}}\mathrm{Dil}_{\beta t}\varphi_{0}(x)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}t;\end{aligned}

the wave packet
\varphi_{0}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
can be chosen
independently of
\beta
, and to satisfy
\mathrm{spt}\widehat{\varphi_{0}}\subseteq B_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}
for any fixed
\mathfrak{r}_{0}<2^{-20}
. The constant
C_{\beta}>0
is
uniformly bounded above and away from
0
for all
\beta\in(0,1]
.

The model wave packet
\varphi
is allowed to be any function in

\Phi^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{r}}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
, the subset of Schwartz
functions with compact Fourier support in
\overline{B_{\mathfrak{r}}}\coloneqq\left[-\mathfrak{r},\mathfrak{r}\right]
for some
\mathfrak{r}>0
. Hölder’s inequality shows that the
pointwise product
(f_{1},f_{2})\mapsto f_{1}(x)f_{2}(x)
satisfies the bounds of

Theorem

1
; thus we concern ourselves only with the second summand on

\mathrm{RHS}{\eqref{eq:BHT-wave-packet-representation}}
.

When
p>1
, we prove
Theorem

1
by using identity

(
1.9
)
and dualizing: we reduce to showing
that

(1.10)

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{1}](\eta,y,t)[\varphi_{0}]\prod_{j=2}^{3}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}(\eta,y,t)[\varphi_{0}]\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}t\right|\lesssim\prod_{j=1}^{3}\|f_{j}\|_{L^{p_{j}}(\mathbb{R})},

holds as long as
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in(1,\infty)
and
\sum_{j=1}^{3}p_{j}^{-1}=1
. The maps
\Gamma_{j}:\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
are given by

(1.11)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{j}(\eta,y,t)\coloneqq\Gamma_{(\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\gamma_{j})}(\eta,y,t)\coloneqq\Big(\alpha_{j}(\eta+\gamma_{j}t^{-1}),y,\beta_{j}t\Big),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &(\alpha_{2},\beta_{2},\gamma_{2})\coloneqq\left(\frac{1}{\beta},\beta,-1\right),\\
&(\alpha_{3},\beta_{3},\gamma_{3})\coloneqq\left(-\frac{1+\beta}{\beta},\beta,\frac{-1}{1+\beta}\right);\end{aligned}

for convenience of notation we set
\Gamma_{1}(\eta,y,t)=(\eta,y,t)
. One
should think of
\Gamma_{j}
as encoding the geometric datum of the degeneration
parameter
\beta\in(0,1]
in terms of the group of symmetries generated by
(
1.7
).

The embedding coefficients
\mathrm{Emb}[f](\eta,y,t)[\varphi]
appearing above
provide a description of a function
f
in terms of the defining symmetries of

\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}
. They are given by

(1.12)

\mathrm{Emb}[f](\eta,y,t)[\varphi]\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(z)\mathrm{Tr}_{y}\mathrm{Mod}_{-\eta}\mathrm{Dil}_{t}\varphi(z)\mathrm{d}z.

An appropriate functional analytic framework to understand the embedded
functions
F=\mathrm{Emb}[f]:\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\times\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is
given by outer Lebesgue spaces introduced in

[
DT15
]
.

Non-uniform (
\beta\gtrsim 1
) variants of the bounds
(
1.10
) can be obtained in this framework from the chain of
inequalities

(1.13)

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}}\prod_{j=1}^{3}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}(\eta,y,t)[\varphi_{0}]\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}t\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big\|\prod_{j=1}^{3}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}[\varphi_{0}]\Big\|_{L^{1}_{\nu_{1}}\not{L}^{1}_{\mu^{1}_{\Theta},\nu_{1}}\mathbb{L}^{(1,1)}_{\Theta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\prod_{j=1}^{3}\Big\|\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}[\varphi_{0}]\Big\|_{L^{p_{j}}_{\nu_{1}}\not{L}^{q_{j},+}_{\mu^{1}_{\Theta},\nu_{1}}\mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{en},j}},

and from the embedding bounds

(1.14)

\left\|\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}\right\|_{L^{p_{j}}_{\nu_{1}}\not{L}^{q_{j},+}_{\mu^{1}_{\Theta},\nu_{1}}\mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{en},j}}\lesssim\|f_{j}\|_{L^{p_{j}}(\mathbb{R})}.

for some appropriately chosen exponents
q_{j}\in(2,\infty]
. The terms
appearing on
\mathrm{RHS}{\eqref{eq:outer-domination:non-uniform}}
are

iterated outer Lebesgue quasi-norms

\|\cdot\|_{L^{p_{j}}_{\nu_{1}}\not{L}^{q_{j},+}_{\mu^{1}_{\Theta},\nu_{1}}\mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{en},j}}
. One should think of
these quasi-norms as appropriate generalizations of product Lebesgue space
norms defined on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
, the parameterization of the symmetries
(
1.7
) of the problem at hand. Unlike in the case of classical
Lebesgue integral norms, the outer measures
\nu_{1}
and
\mu^{1}_{\Theta}

appearing in
(
1.14
)
fail to
satisfy Carathéodory measurability conditions on any non-trivial sets: thus
the need for a generalized integration theory. The symbols
\mathbb{S}_{\operatorname{en},j}
and
\mathbb{L}^{(1,1)}_{\Theta}
appearing in the definition of the outer
Lebesgue quasi-norms are
sizes
, quantities that allow us to control
the magnitude of
\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}
locally.

The two inequalities in (
1.13
) are abstract
functional analytic statements about (iterated) outer Lebesgue quasi-norms: a
Radon-Nikodym -type domination lemma and an outer Hölder inequality.
Inequalities (
1.14
) are bounds on
wave packet embeddings
f\mapsto\mathrm{Emb}[f]\circ\Gamma
. In

[
Ura16
]
, with minor notational
differences, one of the authors formulated inequalities
(
1.14
) using this framework in the
context of the variation norm Carleson operator, deducing them from Di Plinio
and Ou’s ideas in
[
PO18
]
. The
iterated variants of outer Lebesgue spaces (e.g. bounds
(
1.13
)) were initially introduced therein. A
complete study of non-uniform bounds for
\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}
in this framework is
carried out with suitable generalizations in

[
AU20a
]
for Banach-valued functions; the
result there subsumes the non-uniform scalar results above. Originally, the
approach to non-uniform bounds for
\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}
using outer Lebesgue spaces
was carried out in
[
DT15
]
, where the non-iterated
variants of outer Lebesgue quasi-norms have been introduced and were
sufficient to obtain bounds (
1.10
) for
p_{j}\in(2,\infty]
,
j\in\{1,2,3\}
.

The
[
DT15
]
proof of the non-iterated variant of
bound (
1.14
) relies on
Calderón-Zygmund techniques when
p=\infty
and on
orthogonality considerations together with a covering lemma for sets on

\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
when
p=2
. The range
p\in(2,\infty]
is then obtained by
interpolation. To go to
p<2
,

[
PO18
]
made use of a multi-frequency
Calderón-Zygmund decomposition. In

[
AU20a
]
provided an alternative proof of
(
1.14
) in the context of Banach
space-valued spaces by interpolating the result with
p>2
with an endpoint
that does not rely on cancellation at all, thus avoiding Hilbert space
techniques. On the other hand, in the recent result

[
DPF22
]
, Di Plinio and Fragos improve on

[
PO18
]
and obtain an endpoint
embedding result close to
L^{1}
for the embedding bound
(
1.14
) in the context of endpoint
estimates for the Carleson operator. Their result do not follow by
interpolation; they make use Gabor frame techniques and work in a localized
outer Lebesgue endpoint space formally reminiscent of the space

X_{\mu_{\Theta}^{1},\nu_{\beta}}^{q,r,+}
we introduce in

Section

3.4
.

In the current paper, we generalize the framework of outer Lebesgue spaces
and the bounds (
1.14
). However, we
do not prove the analogue of (
1.14
)
directly. With few exceptions, arguments are carried out purely in terms of
properties of embedded functions
F:\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\times\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and corresponding outer Lebesgue quasi-norms; we mainly rely
on a extensions of ideas related to the covering lemma from

[
DT15
]
.

1.3.
Outline of the argument

In this paper, we require a significant extensions of the framework of outer
Lebesgue spaces. A Radon-Nikodym domination type result, as before, allows us
to write

(1.15)

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}}\prod_{j=1}^{3}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}(\eta,y,t)[\varphi_{0}]\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}t\Big|\lesssim\Big\|\prod_{j=1}^{3}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}\Big\|_{L^{1}_{\nu_{1}}\not{L}^{1}_{\mu^{1}_{\Theta},\nu_{1}}\mathbb{I}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}}.

First of all, the sizes we use no longer evaluate the function
\mathrm{Emb}[f]\circ\Gamma
at a fixed
\varphi_{0}\in\Phi^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{r}}
but they track the
dependence of
\mathrm{Emb}[f]\circ\Gamma
on all possible generating wave packets

\varphi\in\Phi^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{r}}
. This is emphasized by the notation

\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}
appearing in the size above; the parameter
N\in\mathbb{N}
controls
the rate of spatial decay of
\varphi
while
\mathfrak{r}>0
governs the support of

\widehat{\varphi}
. In the subsequent discussion and in the entirety of this paper
the specific values of
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathfrak{r}>0
are inessential: inequalities
hold with constants dependent on
N
and
\mathfrak{r}
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
large enough
and all
\mathfrak{r}>0
small enough independently. However, the choice of
N
and

\mathfrak{r}
can be made independently of
\beta\in(0,1]
. Actually, all implicit
constants henceforth are uniformly bounded in
\beta\in(0,1]
.

Continuing with the difference with respect to the non-uniform bound
(
1.13
), we use a much more involved size

(1.16)

\mathbb{I}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}\coloneqq\begin{aligned} &{\mathbb{I}_{\Theta}}^{(l,l,l)}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}+{\mathbb{I}_{\Theta}}^{(o,l,l)}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}\\
&\qquad+{\mathbb{I}_{\Theta}}^{(l,l,o)}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}+{\mathbb{I}_{\Theta}}^{(l,o,l)}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}+{\mathbb{I}_{\Theta}}^{(l,o,o)}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}\end{aligned}

that replaces the simpler size
\mathbb{L}^{(1,1)}_{\Theta}
modeled locally on a
classical
L^{1}
Lebesgue norm.

The outer Lebesgue quasi-norm on the
\mathrm{RHS}{\eqref{eq:RN-domination-BHT}}
is
then divided into two parts. A part with all but one size forming
\mathbb{I}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}
in (
1.16
), can be bound, similarly to
(
1.13
), by a product of outer Lebesgue norms
of the embedded functions
\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]
. It holds that

(1.17)

\displaystyle\Big\|\prod_{j=1}^{3}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}\Big\|_{L^{1}_{\nu_{1}}\not{L}^{1}_{\mu_{\Theta}^{1},\nu_{1}}\left({\mathbb{I}_{\Theta}}^{(l,l,l)}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}+\mathbb{I}^{(o,l,l)}_{\Theta}\Phi^{N}_{\mathfrak{r}}+\mathbb{I}^{(l,o,l)}_{\Theta}\Phi^{N}_{\mathfrak{r}}+\mathbb{I}^{(l,l,o)}_{\Theta}\Phi^{N}_{\mathfrak{r}}\right)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big\|\mathrm{Emb}[f_{1}]\Big\|_{L^{p_{1}}_{\nu_{1}}\not{L}^{q_{1},+}_{\mu_{\Theta}^{\infty},\nu_{1}}\mathbb{F}^{u_{1}}_{\Theta}\Phi_{4\mathfrak{r}}^{N-3}}\prod_{j=2}^{3}\Big\|\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}\Big\|_{L^{p_{j}}_{\nu_{\beta}}X_{\mu_{\Theta}^{1},\nu_{\beta}}^{q_{j},r_{j},+}\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{u_{j}}_{\Gamma_{j}}\Phi_{4\mathfrak{r}}^{N-3}}

for any
p_{j}\in(1,\infty)
with
\sum_{j=1}^{3}p_{j}^{-1}=1
with the
powers
q_{j}
,
r_{j}
, and
u_{j}
chosen appropriately depending on
p_{j}
. We
introduce a new outer measures
\nu_{\beta_{j}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
to deal with the
functions
\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}
with
j\in\{2,3\}
, for which

\beta_{j}=\beta\ll 1
. Bounds (
1.17
)
are deduced from an “atomic estimate” using restricted weak type
interpolation for outer measure Lebesgue spaces
(
Proposition

3.3
). This generalizes the simpler
Hölder inequality for outer Lebesgue spaces used in the non-uniform case of
bounds (
1.13
). We then obtain bounds

(1.18)

\displaystyle\Big\|\prod_{j=1}^{3}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}\Big\|_{L^{1}_{\nu_{1}}\not{L}^{1}_{\mu_{\Theta}^{1},\nu_{1}}\left({\mathbb{I}_{\Theta}}^{(l,l,l)}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}+\mathbb{I}^{(o,l,l)}_{\Theta}\Phi^{N}_{\mathfrak{r}}+\mathbb{I}^{(l,o,l)}_{\Theta}\Phi^{N}_{\mathfrak{r}}+\mathbb{I}^{(l,l,o)}_{\Theta}\Phi^{N}_{\mathfrak{r}}\right)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\prod_{j=1}^{3}\|f_{j}\|_{L^{p_{j}}(\mathbb{R})}

by using (
1.17
) and by showing the
non-uniform (
\beta=1
) embedding bounds

(1.19)

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathrm{Emb}[f_{1}]\Big\|_{L^{p_{1}}_{\nu_{1}}\not{L}^{q_{1},+}_{\mu_{\Theta}^{\infty},\nu_{1}}\mathbb{F}^{u_{1}}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}}\lesssim\|f_{1}\|_{L^{p_{1}}(\mathbb{R})}

and the uniform embedding bounds

(1.20)

\Big\|\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}\Big\|_{L^{p_{j}}_{\nu_{\beta}}X_{\mu_{\Theta}^{1},\nu_{\beta}}^{q_{j},r_{j},+}\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{u_{j}}_{\Gamma_{j}}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}}\lesssim\|f_{j}\|_{L^{p_{j}}(\mathbb{R})}.

Bounds (
1.19
) mostly extend the
available results from
[
AU20a
]
to account
for additional terms in the size
\mathbb{F}_{\Theta}^{u_{1}}
and a larger outer
measure
\mu^{\infty}_{\Theta}
(cfr. (
1.14
)). Bounds
(
1.20
) are an innovation of the current
work. Unlike (
1.19
), they are not
proven directly as in
[
AU20a
]
and

[
DT15
]
by analyzing the function
f_{j}
. Rather, we
obtain (
1.20
) from geometric properties
of the diffeomorphisms
\Gamma_{j}
on the time-frequency-scale space and by
proving a covering lemma directly on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
.

To deal with the outer Lebesgue quasi-norms with the size
\mathbb{I}^{(l,o,o)}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}
we prove the bound

(1.21)

\displaystyle\Big\|\prod_{j=1}^{3}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}\Big\|_{L^{1}_{\nu_{1}}\not{L}^{1}_{\mu_{\Theta}^{1},\nu_{1}}\mathbb{I}^{(l,o,o)}_{\Theta}\Phi^{N}_{\mathfrak{r}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big\|\mathrm{Emb}[f_{1}]\Big\|_{L^{p_{1}}_{\nu_{1}}\not{L}^{q_{1},+}_{\mu_{\Theta}^{\infty},\nu_{1}}\mathbb{F}_{\Theta}^{u_{1}}\Phi_{4\mathfrak{r}}^{N-3}}\Big\|\prod_{j=2}^{3}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}\Big\|_{L^{p_{\times}}_{\nu_{\beta}}X_{\mu_{\Theta}^{1},\nu_{\beta}}^{q_{\times},r_{\times},+}\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{u_{\times}}_{\Gamma_{\times}}\Phi_{4\mathfrak{r}}^{N-3}}

for any
p_{1},p_{\times}\in(1,\infty)
with
p_{1}^{-1}+p_{\times}^{-1}=1
and for an appropriate choice of
q_{1},q_{\times}
,
r_{\times}
, and
u_{1},u_{\times}
. To show

(1.22)

\Big\|\prod_{j=1}^{3}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}\Big\|_{L^{1}_{\nu_{1}}\not{L}^{1}_{\mu_{\Theta}^{1},\nu_{1}}\mathbb{I}^{(l,o,o)}_{\Theta}\Phi^{N}_{\mathfrak{r}}}\lesssim\prod_{j=1}^{3}\|f_{j}\|_{L^{p_{j}}(\mathbb{R})}

requires us, in addition to using
(
1.19
), to prove a bilinear uniform
embedding bound

(1.23)

\Big\|\mathrm{Emb}[f_{2}]\circ\Gamma_{2}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{3}]\circ\Gamma_{3}\Big\|_{L^{p_{\times}}_{\nu_{\beta}}X^{q_{\times},r_{\times},+}_{\mu_{\Theta}^{1},\nu_{\beta}}\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{u_{\times}}_{\Gamma_{\times}}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}}\lesssim\|f_{2}\|_{L^{p_{2}}(\mathbb{R})}\|f_{2}\|_{L^{p_{3}}(\mathbb{R})}.

The product term in
\mathrm{LHS}{\eqref{eq:embedding-bound:bilinear}}
is another new
result of this work. We believe it to be of crucial importance since for any
fixed
f_{1,2,3}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
the term

(1.24)

\Big\|\prod_{j=1}^{3}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}\Big\|_{L^{1}_{\nu_{1}}\not{L}^{1}_{\mu_{\Theta}^{1},\nu_{1}}\mathbb{I}^{(l,o,o)}_{\Theta}\Phi^{N}_{\mathfrak{r}}}

becomes the most relevant one when
\beta\rightarrow 0^{+}
. Furthermore, we
believe that bounds (
1.23
) are intrinsically
bilinear and they cannot be deduced from linear embedding bounds similar to
(
1.20
).

After setting up the appropriate extension of the outer measure Lebesgue space
framework, we prove the following results.

Theorem 2
.

Let
\Gamma_{j}=\Gamma_{(\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\gamma_{j})}
,
j\in\{2,3\}
with
(\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\gamma_{j})
as in
(
1.11
). In particular suppose
1+\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}=0

and
|\gamma_{j}|\geq 1/4
,
j\in\{2,3\}
. The bound

(1.25)

\displaystyle\|F_{1}F_{2}F_{3}\|_{\left(\mathbb{I}^{(l,l,l)}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}+\mathbb{I}^{(o,l,l)}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}+\mathbb{I}^{(l,l,o)}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}+\mathbb{I}^{(l,o,l)}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}\right)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|F_{1}\|_{\mathbb{F}_{\Theta}^{u_{1}}\Phi_{4\mathfrak{r}}^{N-3}}\|F_{2}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\Gamma_{2}}^{u_{2}}\Phi_{4\mathfrak{r}}^{N-3}}\|F_{3}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\Gamma_{3}}^{u_{3}}\Phi_{4\mathfrak{r}}^{N-3}}

holds for any three functions
F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}\in L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}){\otimes\Phi_{4\mathfrak{r}}^{(N-3)}}^{\prime}
as long as
u_{j\in\{1,2,3\}}\in[1,\infty]
with
\sum_{j=1}^{3}u_{j}^{-1}\leq 1
.
Furthermore the bound

(1.26)

\|F_{1}F_{2}F_{3}\|_{\mathbb{I}^{(l,o,o)}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}}\lesssim\|F_{1}\|_{\mathbb{F}_{\Gamma_{1}}^{u_{1}}\Phi_{4\mathfrak{r}}^{(N-5)}}\|F_{2}F_{3}\|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\Gamma_{\times}}^{u_{\times}}\Phi_{4\mathfrak{r}}^{(N-5)}}

holds for any three functions
F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}\in L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}){\otimes\Phi_{4\mathfrak{r}}^{(N-3)}}^{\prime}
as long as
u_{1},u_{\times}\in[1,\infty]
with
u_{1}^{-1}+u_{\times}^{-1}\leq 1
.

The bounds (
1.25
) and (
1.26
) are referred to as
the “single tree estimate” and they serve as the
L^{\infty}
endpoints for
the interpolation procedure to obtain
(
1.17
) and
(
1.21
), respectively.

The second main result of this work consists of proving uniform embedding
bounds.

Theorem 3
.

Let
\Gamma=\Gamma_{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}
be as in (
3.103
). Let
\Theta
be a open interval
with
B_{4}\subsetneq\Theta\subsetneq B_{2^{8}}
, and let

\Theta^{\operatorname{in}}
be a bounded open interval with
B_{2^{-5}}(-\gamma)\subsetneq\Theta^{\operatorname{in}}\subsetneq B_{2^{-3}}(-\gamma)
. Let the
size
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{u}_{\Gamma}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}
be given by

(1.27)

\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{u}_{\Gamma}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}\coloneqq{\beta^{\frac{1}{u}}}\Gamma^{\ast}\mathbb{L}^{(u,\infty)}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}+\Gamma^{\ast}\mathbb{L}^{(u,2)}_{\Theta}\mathfrak{d}_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}+\Gamma^{\ast}\mathbb{L}^{(u,1)}_{(\Theta,\Theta^{\operatorname{in}})}\Box^{N}_{\mathfrak{r}}

The bounds (
1.20
) hold with
\Gamma_{j}=\Gamma
and with
(p_{j},q_{j},r_{j},u_{j})=(p,q,r,u)
for all
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
as long as
p\in(1,\infty]
,
q\in(\max(p^{\prime},2),\infty]
, and
1\leq u<r<q
. The implicit constant is independent of

\Gamma_{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}
and
f
.

Theorem 4
.

Let
\Gamma_{j}=\Gamma_{(\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\gamma_{j})}
,
j\in\{2,3\}
with
(\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\gamma_{j})
as in (
1.11
). Let
\Theta
and

\Theta^{\operatorname{in}}
be as in
Theorem

3
.
Let the size
\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{u}_{\Gamma_{\times}}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}
is
given by

(1.28)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{F}}^{u}_{\Gamma_{\times}}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}\coloneqq\begin{aligned} &\beta^{\frac{1}{u}}\Big(\Gamma_{\times}^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{(\Theta,\Theta^{\operatorname{in}})}^{(u,\infty)}(\Phi\otimes\Phi)_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}+\Gamma_{\times}^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{(\Theta,\Theta^{\operatorname{in}})}^{(u,2)}(\Phi\otimes\mathfrak{d})_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}\Big.\\
&\qquad\Big.+\Gamma_{\times}^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{(\Theta,\Theta^{\operatorname{in}})}^{(u,2)}(\mathfrak{d}\otimes\Phi)_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}\Big)+\Gamma_{\times}^{\ast}{\mathbb{L}}^{(u,1)}_{\Theta}(\mathfrak{d}\otimes\mathfrak{d})_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}+\Gamma_{\times}^{\ast}\mathbb{L}_{(\Theta,\Theta^{\operatorname{in}})}^{(u,1)}\Box_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}\end{aligned}

The bounds (
1.23
) hold for all
f_{2},f_{3}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
as long as
(p_{2},p_{3})\in(1,\infty]
,

p_{\times}^{-1}=p_{2}^{-1}+p_{3}^{-1}
,
q_{\times}^{-1}<\min\left(1-\frac{1}{p_{2}},\frac{1}{2}\right)^{-1}+\min\left(1-\frac{1}{p_{3}},\frac{1}{2}\right)^{-1}
, and
1\leq u_{\times}<r_{\times}<q_{\times}
. The implicit constant is independent of
\beta\in(0,1]
, that determines
(\alpha_{j},\beta_{j},\gamma_{j})
and
\Gamma_{j}
,
j\in\{2,3\}
, and of
f_{2},f_{3}
.

As mentioned before, we do not prove these results directly but rather we
deduce them from the non-uniform bounds
(
1.19
), that in turn follow from

[
AU20a
]
. To do so we make use of covering
lemmata depending on the geometry of the outer measure space
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
. The
arguments involved in the proof of the above results are purely positive and
can be interpreted as boundedness properties of the map
F\mapsto F\circ\Gamma
in terms of outer Lebesgue quasi-norms. As a matter of fact, with few
exceptions, the functions
F\in\mathscr{R}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}){\otimes\Phi^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{\prime}
in play need not be of the form
F=\mathrm{Emb}[f]
for some

f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
. We are hopeful that appropriate refinements
can lead to completely removing such assumptions in the intermediate lemmata.

Finally, we carry out some improvements to the non-uniform embedding bounds
in
[
AU20a
]
to obtain the following
theorem. The improvement from using the outer measure
\mu^{1}_{\Theta}
to

\mu^{\infty}_{\Theta}
is common knowledge in time-scale-frequency analysis
and had to merely be codified using the outer Lebesgue framework. We are not
aware of a prior use of the quantity (size)
\mathbb{J}_{\Theta}^{u}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}
,
appearing as a part of
\mathbb{F}^{u}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}
; it represents a maximal
truncation of a singular integral operator.

Theorem 5
.

Let
\Theta
be an bounded open real
interval with
B_{1}\subsetneq\Theta\subsetneq B_{2^{10}}
. Let the size

\mathbb{F}^{u}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}
be given by

(1.29)

\mathbb{F}^{u}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}\coloneqq\mathbb{L}^{(\infty,\infty)}_{\Theta}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}+\mathbb{L}^{(u,2)}_{\Theta}\mathfrak{d}_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}+\mathbb{L}^{(u,1)}_{\Theta}\Box^{N}_{\mathfrak{r}}+\mathbb{J}_{\Theta}^{u}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}.

The bounds (
1.19
) hold for all
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
as long as
p\in(1,\infty)
,
q\in(\max(p^{\prime},2),\infty)
, and
u\in(1,\infty)
. The implicit constant is
independent of
f
.

We summarize the steps necessary to prove
Theorem

1
.

Proof.

Proof of
Theorem

1

Given the identity (
1.9
), the claim
follows by interpolation of bound (
1.10
) for any fixed

(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\in(1,\infty)^{3}
with
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}p_{j}^{-1}=1
boundedness result
[
Li06
, Theorem 2]
.

To show that the bound (
1.10
) holds we first apply the
bound (
1.15
) to reduce ourselves to showing the bounds

(1.30)

\mathrm{RHS}{\eqref{eq:RN-domination-BHT}}=\mathrm{LHS}{\eqref{eq:iterated-Holder-type-bounds:linear}}+\mathrm{LHS}{\eqref{eq:iterated-Holder-type-bounds:bilinear}}\lesssim\prod_{j=1}^{3}\|f_{j}\|_{L^{p_{j}}(\mathbb{R})}=\mathrm{RHS}{\eqref{eq:BHT-dual-bounds}}.

Let us fix
(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\in(1,\infty)^{3}
.

Bounds (
1.20
) hold for
j\in\{2,3\}
as long as
q_{j}>\max(2,p_{j}^{\prime})
, and
1<u_{j}<r_{j}<q_{j}
. Bounds
(
1.19
) hold as long as
q_{1}>\max(2,p_{1}^{\prime})
and
u_{1}<\infty
. Bounds (
1.23
)
hold with
p_{\times}^{-1}=p_{2}^{-1}+p_{3}^{-2}=1-p_{1}^{-1}
, with

q_{\times}^{-1}=q_{2}^{-1}+q_{3}^{-1}
, with
r_{\times}^{-1}=r_{2}^{-1}+r_{3}^{-1}>q_{\times}^{-1}
. From this we get that

(1.31)

\mathrm{RHS}{\eqref{eq:iterated-Holder-type-bounds:linear}}+\mathrm{RHS}{\eqref{eq:iterated-Holder-type-bounds:bilinear}}\lesssim\prod_{j=1}^{3}\|f_{j}\|_{L^{p_{j}}(\mathbb{R})}.

It remains to show that bounds (
1.17
)
and (
1.21
) hold for an appropriate
choice of
1<u_{j}<r_{j}<q_{j}
. Let
q_{j}=\max(2,p_{j}^{\prime})+4\varepsilon

and let
r_{j}=2+2\varepsilon^{\prime}
and
u_{j}=2+\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}
,
j\in\{2,3\}
, for some small
\varepsilon>\varepsilon^{\prime}>\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}>0
to
be determined later. It then holds that
\sum_{j=1}^{3}q_{j}^{-1}>1
if

\varepsilon>0
is chosen small enough. We also have
2<u_{j}<r_{j}<q_{j}

for
j\in\{2,3\}
for all sufficiently small choices of
\varepsilon^{\prime}>\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}>0
. Fix
u_{1}=(1-u_{2}^{-1}-u_{3}^{-1})^{-1}<\infty
.

Note that
p_{2}^{-1}+p_{3}^{-2}=1-p_{1}^{-1}<1
while
r_{2}^{-1}+r_{3}^{-1}=\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon^{\prime}}>1-p_{1}^{-1}
if
\varepsilon^{\prime}>0
is chosen small enough. Under these conditions the bound
(
1.17
) and the bound
(
1.21
) hold (see

Proposition

5.4
).
∎

1.4.
Structure of the paper

In
Section

2
we show that the identity
(
1.9
) holds.

In
Section

3
we discuss the functional framework used to deal
with embedded functions
F:\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\times\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
for which an important example are functions of the form
F=\mathrm{Emb}[f]
.
In
Section

3.1
we discuss the general outer Lebesgue space
framework and prove abstract functional analytic results. We recall the
necessary facts and prove a restricted weak type interpolation theorem for
these spaces. In
Section

3.2
we discuss the outer
Lebesgue space structure specifically on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
that we will be using
throughout the paper. In particular, we introduce the outer measures

\mu^{1}_{\Theta}
and
\mu^{\infty}_{\Theta}
, trees, and sizes. We first
discuss the sizes used to locally control the magnitude of embedded functions

F:\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\times\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and then we introduce
the size
\mathbb{I}_{\Theta}
appearing in (
1.15
) adapted to
controlling the term
\prod_{j=1}^{3}\mathrm{Emb}[f_{j}]\circ\Gamma_{j}
i.e. the
product of several functions
F_{j}:\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\times\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
. In
Section

3.3
we discuss how the former sizes
must be modified when dealing with compositions with diffeomorphisms:
F\mapsto F\circ\Gamma
. We also define the product sizes used to deal with
functions
F_{\times}=F_{2}F_{3}
appearing in
(
1.21
) and
(
1.23
). In
Section

3.4
we
introduce the strip outer measures
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{\beta}
, and we construct

localized outer Lebesgue quasi-norms
based on a localization
procedure of the already defined outer Lebesgue quasi-norms. We thus give
meaning to the symbols
\not{L}^{q,+}_{\mu_{\Theta}^{\infty},\nu_{1}}
,
\not{L}^{q,+}_{\mu_{\Theta}^{1},\nu_{\beta}}
, and
X^{q,r,+}_{\mu^{1}_{\Theta},\nu_{\beta}}
.

In
Section

4
we prove
Theorem

2
, the single tree estimate.
This is the starting point for showing bounds
(
1.17
) and
(
1.21
) in the following section. The
technically involved proof can be seen as the motivation for the specific
choice of sizes introduced in
Section

3
.

We begin
Section

5
by showing that (
1.15
) holds
and by doing so we justify the choice of the size
\mathbb{I}_{\Theta}
introduced in

Section

3.2
. We then prove bounds
(
1.17
) and
(
1.21
) by using the restricted weak
type interpolation statement from
Section

3.2
.
The bulk of this part consists of deducing the atomic bound necessary to apply
interpolation (
Proposition

3.3
) from the single
tree estimate (
Theorem

2
).

In
Section

6
we prove
Theorem

5
.
We begin by referencing available non-uniform results that apply to
\mathrm{Emb}[f_{1}]
. We then prove the necessary improvements consisting of controlling the
new term
\mathbb{J}^{u}_{\Theta}
, that is part of the size
\mathbb{F}^{u}_{\Theta},
and
improving the localized outer Lebesgue norm
\not{L}^{q,+}_{\mu_{\Theta}^{1},\nu_{1}}
to
\not{L}^{q,+}_{\mu_{\Theta}^{\infty},\nu_{1}}
. In doing so we loose
the
p=\infty
endpoint for the embedding.

In
Section

7
we prove

Theorem

3
. We begin by proving two
boundedness results for the map
F\mapsto F\circ\Gamma
in terms of outer
Lebesgue quasi-norms. The two results, encoded in

Proposition

7.1
and

Proposition

7.2
, are expressed in terms of two
auxiliary Lebesgue quasi-norms, that do not appear in

Theorem

3
. Next, in

Lemma

7.3
we show that as long as
F=\mathrm{Emb}[f]
the
two auxiliary Lebesgue quasi-norms control the ones appearing in

Theorem

3
. Then, by appealing to

Theorem

5
we deduce that
Theorem

3
holds.

Section

7.1
is dedicated to proving

Proposition

7.1
,
Section

7.2
is
dedicated to proving
Proposition

7.2
, and

Section

7.3
is dedicated to proving

Lemma

7.3
.
Proposition

7.1
and

Proposition

7.2
are a consequence of two purely
geometric covering lemmata on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
that can be found in the respective
sections.

In
Section

8
we prove

Theorem

4
that follows along the same lines
of the proof
Theorem

3
. Most of the arguments
will rely on the results of
Section

7
.

1.5.
Notation

We denote by
B_{r}(c)
the Euclidean open ball of radius
r
. The dimension
can usually be extrapolated from context. If
c=0
then we use the shorthand

B_{r}
. We use the bracket notation:

(1.32)

\langle x\rangle\coloneqq\left({1+x^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Given two subsets
A,B
of a metric space we set
\mathrm{dist}(A,B)\coloneqq\inf_{a\in A,b\in B}\mathrm{dist}(a,b)
and
\mathrm{dist}(a,B)\coloneqq\inf_{b\in B}\mathrm{dist}(a,b)
. Given
A\subset\mathbb{R}
we denote by
|A|
its Lebesgue measure. The
Schwartz space, denoted by
\mathscr{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
, is the space of

C^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
functions for which

(1.33)

\Big\|\langle x\rangle^{N}f\Big\|_{C^{N}}<+\infty\qquad\forall N\in\mathbb{N}.

We denote
\mathbb{R}_{+}\coloneqq(0,+\infty)
and
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{+}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Given a set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
we set

(1.34)

\displaystyle E+x=\{y+x:y\in E\},\qquad\lambda E=\{\lambda y:y\in E\}.

We denote the total derivative in a variable (
z
) by
\mathrm{d}_{z}
; the partial
derivative in the
i^{\operatorname{th}}
coordinate is denoted by
\partial_{i}
. We
use the convention on the order of variables that allows us to use more
descriptive notation:

(1.35)

\displaystyle\partial_{\eta}\equiv\partial_{\theta}\equiv\partial_{1},\qquad\partial_{y}\equiv\partial_{\zeta}\equiv\partial_{2},\qquad\partial_{t}\equiv\partial_{\sigma}\equiv\partial_{3}.

We do not use
\varphi^{\prime}
to denote the derivative of a function
\varphi
: we prefer

\partial\varphi
and leave the notation
\varphi^{\prime}
to denote another function that
is not necessarily related to
\varphi
.

The Fourier transform of a function
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is given by

(1.36)

\mathfrak{F}f\left(\widehat{z}\right)\equiv\widehat{f}\left(\widehat{z}\right)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(z)e^{-2\pi iz\widehat{z}}\mathrm{d}z.

If
f
depends on multiple variables we will denote the variable in which we
take the Fourier transform by writing
\mathfrak{F}_{z}
in place of
\mathfrak{F}
. We
often denote the “dual” variable to
z
by
\widehat{z}
. Given a functions
f

we set
f^{\vee}(z)=f(-z)
.

We write
A\lesssim B
if there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that

A\leq CB
. The constant may implicitly depend on some parameters, as judged
from context, but it will always be independent of
\beta\in(0,1]
in our
problem. If we want to emphasize that the constant
C
may depend on a
parameter
p
, we write
A\lesssim_{p}B
. We generally allow the constant
C

in our bounds to change value from one line to the next. We say that a
quantity
A
is bounded if
|A|\lesssim 1
and we say it is bounded away
from
0
if
|A|^{-1}\lesssim 1
.

Given an equality or inequality
(\star)
we denote by
\mathrm{LHS}{}(\star)
and

\mathrm{RHS}{}(\star)
the expressions on the left and right hand sides of

(\star)
, respectively.

If
K\in D^{\prime}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
we abuse the integral to denote duality
pairing by writing
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}fK
or
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(z)K(z)\mathrm{d}z
. The
distribution
\delta
is given by

(1.37)

\int f(z)\delta(z)\mathrm{d}z=f(0).

Given
\varphi\in C^{1}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}\right)
we set

(1.38)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(z)\delta(\varphi(z))\mathrm{d}z=\int_{\varphi^{-1}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{f(z)}{|\nabla\varphi(z)|}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(z)

as long as
\nabla\varphi\neq 0
on
\varphi^{-1}(0)
. The above can be
interpreted using the limiting procedure if
f\in C^{0}
by setting

(1.39)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(z)\delta(\varphi(z))\mathrm{d}z=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(z)\delta_{\varepsilon}(\varphi(z))\mathrm{d}z

where
\delta_{\varepsilon}(z)=\varepsilon^{-1}\delta_{1}(z/\varepsilon)
for some
\delta_{1}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
with

\int\delta_{1}(x)\mathrm{d}x=1
. We do not elaborate on the formal theory of
such integrals; the interested reader is directed to

[
Fed96
]
.

1.6.

for their encouragement, suggestions, and insightful questions.

[
He98
]

2.
Wave packet representation.

Proposition 2.1
.

For any
\mathfrak{r}_{0}<2^{-20}
there exists

\varphi\in\Phi^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}
i.e.
\varphi_{0}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})

with
\mathrm{spt}\widehat{\varphi}_{0}\subset\left[-\mathfrak{r}_{0},\mathfrak{r}_{0}\right]
, such that
identity (
1.9
) holds for all
\beta\in(0,1]
and all
f_{j}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
,
j\in\{1,2,3\}
with
a real constant
C_{\beta}>0
uniformly bounded from above and away from

0
. The integral in (
1.9
) converges
absolutely and locally uniformly in
x
. In particular, for any
R\gg 1

and
\varepsilon>0
there exists a compact set
\mathbb{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}

such that

(2.1)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\setminus\mathbb{K}}\Big|\mathrm{Emb}[f_{1}](\eta,y,t)[\varphi_{0}]\mathrm{Emb}[f_{2}](\beta^{-1}\eta-(\beta t)^{-1},y,\beta t)[\varphi_{0}]\Big.

\displaystyle\times\Big.\mathrm{Tr}_{y}\mathrm{Mod}_{\frac{1+\beta}{\beta}\eta-(\beta t)^{-1}}\mathrm{Dil}_{\beta t}\varphi_{0}(x)\Big|\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}t<\varepsilon

for all
x\in B_{R}
.

Proof.

Using the Fourier inversion formula we can write that

(2.2)

\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}[f_{1},f_{2}](x)\begin{aligned} &=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{\varepsilon<|t|<\varepsilon^{-1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{f_{1}}(\xi_{1})\widehat{f_{2}}(\xi_{2})e^{2\pi i(\xi_{1}(x-t)+\xi_{2}(x+\beta t))}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\\
&=-\pi i\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{f_{1}}(\xi_{1})\widehat{f_{2}}(\xi_{2})e^{2\pi i(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})x}\mathrm{sgn}(\xi_{1}-\beta\xi_{2})\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}.\end{aligned}

We used that

(2.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\int_{|t|>\varepsilon}e^{-2\pi i\xi t}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}=-\pi i\mathrm{sgn}(\xi)\qquad\forall\xi\neq 0,

\displaystyle\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\sup_{\xi}\Big|\int_{|t|>\varepsilon}e^{-2\pi i\xi t}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\Big|<+\infty.

Thus

(2.4)

\frac{1}{2}f_{1}(x)f_{2}(x)-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}[f_{1},f_{2}](x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{f_{1}}(\xi_{1})\widehat{f_{2}}(\xi_{2})e^{2\pi i(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})x}{\mathbbm{1}_{\xi_{1}-\beta\xi_{2}>0}}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}.

Let
\widehat{\varphi_{0}}\in C^{\infty}_{c}\left(B_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}\right)
be real-valued,
even, with
0\leq\widehat{\varphi}_{0}\left(\widehat{z}\right)\leq 1
, and with

\widehat{\varphi}_{0}\left(\widehat{z}\right)=1
for
\widehat{z}\in B_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}/2}
. We
show that the multiplier
{\mathbbm{1}_{\xi_{1}-\beta\xi_{2}>0}}
admits the
representation

(2.5)

\mathbbm{1}_{(\xi_{1}-\beta\xi_{2})>0}=\frac{1}{C_{\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{\varphi}_{0}(t\xi_{1}-\theta)\widehat{\varphi}_{0}(\beta t\xi_{2}-\theta+1)\widehat{\varphi}_{0}(\beta t(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})-(1+\beta)\theta+1)\mathrm{d}\theta\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t};

the integral converges uniformly for
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
on any compact subset
of

\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\left\{(\beta\xi,\xi),\xi\in\mathbb{R}\right\}
. A change
of variables
\theta^{\prime}=\theta-t\xi_{1}
gives

(2.6)

m_{t,\beta}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\begin{aligned} &\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{\varphi}_{0}(t\xi_{1}-\theta)\widehat{\varphi}_{0}(\beta t\xi_{2}-\theta+1)\widehat{\varphi}_{0}(\beta t(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})-(1+\beta)\theta+1)\mathrm{d}\theta\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{\varphi}_{0}(-\theta)\widehat{\varphi}_{0}(-\widetilde{\xi}-\theta+1)\widehat{\varphi}_{0}(-\widetilde{\xi}-(1+\beta)\theta+1)\mathrm{d}\theta=m(\widetilde{\xi})\end{aligned}

with
\widetilde{\xi}\coloneqq t(\xi_{1}-\beta\xi_{2})
. The integrand vanishes
unless
\theta\in B_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}\cap B_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}(1-\widetilde{\xi})
and is

1
when
\theta\in B_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}/4}\cap B_{\mathfrak{r}_{0}/4}(1-\widetilde{\xi})
. This shows that

(2.7)

\frac{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}{4}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\frac{\mathfrak{r}_{0}}{4}}(1)}(\widetilde{\xi})\leq m(\widetilde{\xi})\leq\mathfrak{r}_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{2\mathfrak{r}_{0}}(1)}(\widetilde{\xi}).

Using the change of variables
t^{\prime}=t\widetilde{\xi}
if
\widetilde{\xi}>0
and
support considerations if
\widetilde{\xi}\leq 0
we obtain that

(2.8)

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}m_{t,\beta}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}m(t(\xi_{1}-\beta\xi_{2}))\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}=C_{\varphi,\beta}\mathbbm{1}_{(0+\infty)}(t(\xi_{1}-\beta\xi_{2}))

with
C_{\varphi,\beta}\in\left(\frac{\mathfrak{r}_{0}^{2}}{8},8\mathfrak{r}_{0}^{2}\right)
. Using this identity and the Fourier inversion formula we can write

(2.9)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{f}_{1}(\xi_{1})\widehat{f}_{2}(\xi_{2}){\mathbbm{1}_{(\xi_{1}-\beta\xi_{2})>0}}e^{2\pi i(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})x}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{C_{\varphi,\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\widehat{f}_{1}(\xi_{1})\widehat{f}_{2}(\xi_{2}){\mathbbm{1}_{(\xi_{1}-\beta\xi_{2})>0}}e^{2\pi i(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})x}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\mathrm{d}\theta\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{C_{\varphi,\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f_{1}\ast\mathrm{Dil}_{t}\mathrm{Mod}_{\theta}\varphi_{0}(y_{1})f_{2}\ast\mathrm{Dil}_{\beta t}\mathrm{Mod}_{\theta-1}\varphi_{0}(y_{2})\\
&\qquad\times\mathrm{Dil}_{\beta t}\mathrm{Mod}_{(1+\beta)\theta-1}\varphi(y_{3})e^{2\pi i(\xi_{1}(x-y_{1}-y_{3})+\xi_{2}(x-y_{2}-y_{3}))}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\mathrm{d}y_{1}\mathrm{d}y_{2}\mathrm{d}y_{3}\mathrm{d}\theta\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{C_{\varphi,\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{1}\ast\mathrm{Dil}_{t}\mathrm{Mod}_{\theta}\varphi_{0}(y_{1})f_{2}\ast\mathrm{Dil}_{\beta t}\mathrm{Mod}_{\theta-1}\varphi_{0}(y_{2})\\
&\quad\times\mathrm{Dil}_{\beta t}\mathrm{Mod}_{(1+\beta)\theta-1}\varphi_{0}(y_{3})\delta(x-y_{1}-y_{3})\delta(x-y_{2}-y_{3})\mathrm{d}y_{1}\mathrm{d}y_{2}\mathrm{d}y_{3}\mathrm{d}\theta\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{C_{\varphi,\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}}f_{1}\ast\mathrm{Dil}_{t}\mathrm{Mod}_{\theta}\varphi_{0}(y)f_{2}\ast\mathrm{Dil}_{\beta t}\mathrm{Mod}_{\theta-1}\varphi_{0}(y)\\
&\qquad\times\mathrm{Tr}_{y}\mathrm{Dil}_{\beta t}\mathrm{Mod}_{(1+\beta)\theta-1}\varphi_{0}(x)\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}y\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{C_{\varphi,\beta}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}}f_{1}\ast\mathrm{Dil}_{t}\mathrm{Mod}_{\theta}\varphi_{0}(y)f_{2}\ast\mathrm{Dil}_{\beta t}\mathrm{Mod}_{\theta-1}\varphi_{0}(y)\\
&\qquad\times\mathrm{Tr}_{y}\mathrm{Mod}_{\frac{1+\beta}{\beta}\eta-(\beta t)^{-1}}\mathrm{Dil}_{\beta t}\varphi_{0}(x)\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}y\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t}.\end{aligned}

The detailed verification of the validity of the above manipulations is
omitted. To justify the convergence claim let us first suppose that
\mathrm{spt}\widehat{f_{1}},\mathrm{spt}\widehat{f_{2}}\subset B_{S}
for some
S\gg 1
. For
t>\frac{1}{100S}
and
x\in B_{R}
it holds that

(2.10)

\displaystyle\left|f_{1}\ast\mathrm{Dil}_{t}\mathrm{Mod}_{\theta}\varphi_{0}(y)\right|+\left|f_{2}\ast\mathrm{Dil}_{\beta t}\mathrm{Mod}_{\theta-1}\varphi_{0}(y)\right|\lesssim_{\varphi,\beta}t^{-1}(\|f_{1}\|_{L^{1}}+\|f_{2}\|_{L^{1}}),

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{Tr}_{y}\mathrm{Mod}_{\frac{1+\beta}{\beta}\eta-(\beta t)^{-1}}\mathrm{Dil}_{\beta t}\widehat{\varphi}_{0}(x)\right|\lesssim_{\varphi}t^{-1}{\Big\langle\frac{|y|-R}{t}\Big\rangle^{-100}}

while for
t<\frac{1}{100S}
either
\widehat{z}\rightarrow\widehat{\varphi_{0}}\left(\widehat{z}-\theta\right)
or
\widehat{z}\rightarrow\widehat{\varphi_{0}}\left(\widehat{z}-\theta-1\right)
vanishes on
B_{\frac{1}{100}}
so

(2.11)

f_{1}\ast\mathrm{Dil}_{t}\mathrm{Mod}_{\theta}\varphi_{0}(y)=0\qquad\text{or}\qquad f_{2}\ast\mathrm{Dil}_{\beta t}\mathrm{Mod}_{\theta-1}\varphi_{0}(y)=0.

The claim for
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
without restrictions on
the support of the Fourier transform follows by standard approximation
arguments.
∎

3.
Outer Lebesgue spaces and the time-frequency-scale
space.

In this section we discuss outer Lebesgue spaces and quasi-norms in general
and, specifically, their details in the case of the time-scale-frequency space

\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
. We begin by discussion embedded functions i.e. functions of the form

\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}\times\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathrm{Emb}[f](\eta,y,t)[\varphi]
that are the main actors of our functional analytic framework.

We define the Banach space of band-limited wave packets of order
N\in\mathbb{N}

and frequency radius
\mathfrak{r}>0
by setting

(3.1)

\Phi^{N}_{\mathfrak{r}}\coloneqq\left\{\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}):\hskip 2.70004pt\widehat{\varphi}\in C^{N}(\mathbb{R}),\mathrm{spt}\widehat{\varphi}\subseteq\overline{B_{\mathfrak{r}}}\right\}

with an associated norm
\|\varphi\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}}=\left\|\widehat{\varphi}\right\|_{C^{N}}
; we set
\Phi^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{r}}\coloneqq\bigcap_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N}
. We say that
F\in\mathscr{R}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})\otimes^{k}{\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}}^{\prime}
if the map
\left(\Phi^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{r}}\right)^{k}\ni(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k})\mapsto F(\cdot)[\varphi_{1},\ldots\varphi_{k}]\in\mathscr{R}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})
is
k
-linear and is valued in signed Radon measures on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
.
Recall that a Radon measure is a measure that is locally finite and inner and
outer regular. In the special case of
k=0
, the space
\mathscr{R}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})\otimes^{0}{\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}}^{\prime}=\mathscr{R}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})
is simply to the space of

\mathbb{C}
-valued Radon measures on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
. When
k=1
we omit
k
from the
notation and simply write
\mathscr{R}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}){\otimes\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}}^{\prime}
. We denote by
L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})\otimes^{k}{\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}}^{\prime}
the subspaces of
\mathscr{R}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+})\otimes^{k}{\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}}^{\prime}
given by functions such that
for every compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
there exists
N_{K}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(3.2)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\sup_{\text{\scriptsize{$\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k}$}}}}\Big\|F(\eta,y,t)[\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k}]\Big\|_{L^{\infty}(K)}<\infty,

where the upper bound is taken over
\varphi_{j}\in\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}
with
\|\varphi_{j}\|_{\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{N_{K}}}\leq 1
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
. For any
fixed
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k}\in\Phi^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{r}}
we have identified the
function
F(\eta,y,t)[\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k}]
with the measure
F(\eta,y,t)[\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k}]\mathrm{d}\eta\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}t
. According to this
notation,
\mathrm{Emb}[f]\in L^{\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}){\otimes\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}}^{\prime}
for any
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
. If
\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}>\mathfrak{r}
then by restricting we can identify a function
F\in\mathscr{R}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}){\otimes\Phi^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}}}^{\prime}
with a function
F\in\mathscr{R}(\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}){\otimes\Phi^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{\prime}
. Analogously to the above we
can define
\mathscr{R}\left(\mathbb{X}\right){\otimes\Phi^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{r}^{\prime}}}^{\prime}
for any
locally compact topological space
\mathbb{X}
.

3.1.
Generalities of outer measure
spaces

We now provide a with brief overview of the construction and properties of
general outer Lebesgue spaces. Specific instances for the space
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
of
all the objects introduced here will be discussed in the next section. The
reader may want to always assume that
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+}
as that is the context in
which we will be using the subsequent construction. Generally, let
\mathbb{X}
be a
locally compact topological space. An outer measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{X}
is a
function
\mu:2^{\mathbb{X}}\rightarrow[0,+\infty]
that is

\sigma
-subadditive. A
\sigma
-generating collection on
\mathbb{X}
is a
collection
\mathbb{B}\subset\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X})
of Borel sets
B
with
\mu(B)<\infty
such that
\mathbb{X}
can be written as a union of countably many elements
of
\mathbb{B}
and such that
\mu(E)
for any
E\subset\mathbb{X}
can be recovered by
countable coverings of elements of
\mathbb{B}
:

(3.3)

\mu(E)=\inf\Big\{\sum_{B\in\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{B}}\mu(B):E\supseteq\bigcup_{B\in\mathcal{B}\subset\mathbb{B}}B\Big\}.

We denote countable unions of
\mathbb{B}
by

(3.4)

\mathbb{B}^{\cup}\coloneqq\Big\{\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}B_{n}\text{ with }B_{n}\in\mathbb{B}\Big\}.

Let
\mathscr{R}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)\otimes^{k}{\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}}^{\prime}
be defined as
in the beginning of
Section

3
, that we refer to, by a light abuse
of lexicon, as “functions” on
\mathbb{X}
. A size is a positive norm-like
functional
\mathbb{S}:\mathscr{R}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)\otimes^{k}{\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}}^{\prime}\rightarrow[0,+\infty]
with the following properties. First of all it
possesses the properties of a quasi-norm:

(3.5)

\|\lambda F\|_{\mathbb{S}}=|\lambda|\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}\qquad\forall F\in\mathscr{R}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)\otimes^{k}{\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}}^{\prime},

and there exists
\operatorname{Tri}_{\mathbb{S}}\geq 1
such that

(3.6)

\|F_{1}+F_{2}\|_{\mathbb{S}}\leq\operatorname{Tri}_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\|F_{1}\|_{\mathbb{S}}+\|F_{2}\|_{\mathbb{S}}\right),\quad\forall F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathscr{R}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)\otimes^{k}{\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}}^{\prime}.

We do not require separation and allow functions
F\neq 0
with
\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}=0
. We require monotonicity in the following form:

(3.7)

\left\|\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{X}\backslash E}F\right\|_{\mathbb{S}}\leq\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}},\quad\forall F\in\mathscr{R}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)\otimes^{k}{\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}}^{\prime},\quad\forall E\in\mathbb{B}^{\cup}.

Finally, we ask that
\mathbb{S}
be regular: if
E_{n}^{+}\in\mathbb{B}^{\cup}
is an
increasing sequence of sets and
E^{-}_{n}\in\mathbb{B}^{\cup}
is a decreasing
sequences of sets such that
\bigcup_{n}E_{n}^{+}\setminus E_{n}^{-}=\mathbb{X}
then

(3.8)

\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}\lesssim\liminf_{n}\left\|\mathbbm{1}_{E_{n}^{+}\setminus E_{n}^{-}}F\right\|_{\mathbb{S}}.

We call the tuple
\left(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{B},\mu,\mathbb{S}\right)
an outer Lebesgue space.
Sizes and measures can be combined via positive linear combinations. Two sizes

\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
are equivalent if
\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}\approx\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}^{\prime}}
and
similarly two outer measures
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are equivalent if
\mu(E)\approx\mu^{\prime}(E)
for all
E\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X})
.

Given an outer Lebesgue space, we define outer Lebesgue quasi-norms by
combining the notions of size and outer measures. The
\lambda
-superlevel
measure of a function
F\in\mathscr{R}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)\otimes^{k}{\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}}^{\prime}
is given by

(3.9)

\mu(\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}>\lambda)\coloneqq\inf\left\{\mu(E):E\in\mathbb{B}^{\cup},\hskip 2.70004pt\|\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus E}F\|_{\mathbb{S}}\leq\lambda\right\}.

This quantity, in general, is not a measure of any specific set, but rather it
is an interpolating quantity between the outer measure
\mu
and the size

\mathbb{S}
. The notion above encodes the optimal way of splitting
F
into two
parts:
F=\mathbbm{1}_{E}F+\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus E}F
with
E\in\mathbb{B}^{\cup}
so that

\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus E}F
has controlled size and
\mathbbm{1}_{E}F
is supported on a
set of controlled measure.

Given
F\in\mathscr{R}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)\otimes^{k}{\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}}^{\prime}
,
its outer Lebesgue quasi-norms and their weak variants are given by

(3.10)

\displaystyle\|F\|_{L_{\mu}^{p}\mathbb{S}}\coloneqq\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\mu(\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}>\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}})\hskip 1.69998pt\frac{\mathrm{d}{}\lambda}{\lambda}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

\displaystyle\|F\|_{L_{\mu}^{p,\infty}\mathbb{S}}\coloneqq{\left(\sup_{\lambda>0}\lambda\mu(\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}>\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}})\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}},\qquad\forall p\in(0,\infty)

\displaystyle\|F\|_{L_{\mu}^{\infty}\mathbb{S}}\coloneqq\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}.

The quantities above vanish only if
\mu(\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}>0)=0
so we quotient
out such functions similarly to how one does in classical Lebesgue theory.
Using the monotonicity property (
3.7
), one can check
that the quantities above are indeed quasi-norms with

(3.11)

\|F_{1}+F_{2}\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}\leq\operatorname{Tri}_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}\left(\|F_{1}\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}+\|F_{2}\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}\right),\qquad\operatorname{Tri}_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}\leq 2^{1+\frac{1}{p}}\operatorname{Tri}_{\mathbb{S}}.

The quasi-norms
L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}
are themselves regular for any
p\in(0,\infty)
since

(3.12)

\mu(\|\mathbbm{1}_{E_{n}^{+}\setminus E_{n}^{-}}F\|_{\mathbb{S}}>2\lambda)\lesssim\liminf_{n}\mu(\|\mathbbm{1}_{E_{n}^{+}\setminus E_{n}^{-}}F\|_{\mathbb{S}}>\lambda)

for any sequence of sets
E_{n}^{+},E^{-}_{n}\in\mathbb{B}^{\cup}
(respectively
increasing and decreasing) such that
\bigcup_{n}E_{n}^{+}\setminus E_{n}^{-}=\mathbb{X}
.

We write
F\in L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}
if
F\in\mathscr{R}\left(\mathbb{X}\right)\otimes^{k}{\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}}^{\prime}
and
\|F\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}<\infty
; the space

\mathbb{X}
and the values of
k
and
\mathfrak{r}
are encoded in the domains for the
size
\mathbb{S}
and the measure
\mathbb{X}
and thus can be deduced without being
explicitly specified.

We now summarize some of the properties of outer Lebesgue quasi-norms relevant
for our work. First of all, outer Lebesgue spaces are a quasi-normed space; in
general, if
\|\cdot\|:X\rightarrow[0,+\infty]
is a function on a
vector space
X
that satisfies

(3.13)

\|v_{1}+v_{2}\|\leq\operatorname{Tri}_{\|\cdot\|}(\|v_{1}\|+\|v_{2}\|)

then for any
K\in\mathbb{N}
one has

(3.14)

\Big\|\sum_{k=1}^{K}v_{k}\Big\|\leq C_{\|\cdot\|}\Big(\sum_{k=1}^{K}k^{C_{\|\cdot\|}}\|v_{k}\|\Big)

for some
C_{\|\cdot\|}>0
depending only on
\operatorname{Tri}_{\|\cdot\|}
.
In particular, for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
C_{\|\cdot\|,\varepsilon}
such that

(3.15)

\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K}v_{k}\right\|\leq C_{\|\cdot\|,\varepsilon}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}2^{\varepsilon k}\|v_{k}\|\right).

We omit the proof of this fact; it relies on a dyadic partitioning argument.

Outer Lebesgue quasi-norms satisfy many of the properties that hold for
classical Lebesgue spaces.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{B},\mu,\mathbb{S})
be an outer space.
If
\overline{\mu}
is another outer measure then for any
p\in(0,\infty)
it
holds that

(3.16)

\|F\|_{L^{p}_{\overline{\mu}}\mathbb{S}}\leq\sup_{E\in\mathbb{B}^{\cup}}\left(\frac{\overline{\mu}(E)}{\mu(E)}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\|F\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}

(the bound is non-trivial only if
\mu(E)=0\Longrightarrow\overline{\mu}(E)=0
). The Chebyshev inequality holds: for any
p\in(0,\infty)
it holds
that

(3.17)

\|F\|_{L^{p,\infty}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}\lesssim\|F\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}\qquad\forall F\in L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}.

On finite measure spaces, outer Lebesgue quasi-norms are nested: for any
p_{-},p_{+}\in(0,\infty]
with
p_{-}\leq p_{+}
it holds that

(3.18)

\|F\|_{L^{p_{-}}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}\lesssim\mu\left(\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}>0\right)^{\frac{1}{p_{-}}-\frac{1}{p_{+}}}\|F\|_{L^{p_{+},\infty}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}

for any
F\in L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}
with an implicit constant independent of
F
.
Outer Lebesgue quasi-norms are log-convex in the exponent
p
. Given any any

p_{0},p_{1}\in(0,\infty]
and any
\theta\in(0,1)
it holds that

(3.19)

\|F\|_{L^{p_{\theta}}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}\leq C_{p_{0},p_{1},\theta}\|F\|_{L^{p_{0},\infty}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}^{1-\theta}\|F\|_{L^{p_{1,\infty}}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}^{\theta},\qquad\forall F\in L^{p_{0},\infty}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}\cap L^{p_{1},\infty}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}.

with
p_{\theta}^{-1}\coloneqq(1-\theta)p_{0}^{-1}+\theta p_{1}^{-1}
.

We omit the proof of this statement as it the same as for classical Lebesgue
spaces and relies on the super-level set representation
(
3.10
) of the (outer) integral. The properties above
motivate the definition of a
L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}
-atom and an associated
decomposition statement for functions
F\in L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}
.

Proposition 3.2

(Atomic decomposition)
.

Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{B},\mu,\mathbb{S})
be an outer
space. Given
p\in(0,\infty]
, an
L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}
-atom is a function
F\in L^{\infty}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}
such that

(3.20)

\mu\left(\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}>0\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\|F\|_{L^{\infty}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}\leq 1.

Given any
F\in L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}
there exists a decreasing sequence of sets

A_{k}\subset A_{k-1}\subset\mathbb{X}
such that

(3.21)

\lim_{N}\Big\|F-\sum_{k=-N}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta A^{k}}F\Big\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}=0

with
\Delta A_{k}\coloneqq A_{k-1}\backslash A_{k}
. The sets
\Delta A_{k}
are
such that

(3.22)

\left\|\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta A_{k}}F\right\|_{\mathbb{S}}\leq 2^{\frac{k}{p}},\qquad\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu(\Delta A_{k})2^{k}\approx\|F\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}^{p},

and
A_{k}=\emptyset
if
\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}<2^{k/p}.
In particular, for
some
C\gtrsim 1
, the functions
\frac{1}{C}\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta A_{k}}F}{\|F\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}}
are
L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}
-atoms. Furthermore, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}

(3.23)

\displaystyle F-\Big(\sum_{k=-N}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta A^{k}}F\Big)=\mathbbm{1}_{A_{N}}F+\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus A_{-N-1}}F,

and the bounds

(3.24)

\left\|\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus A_{-N-1}}F\right\|_{\mathbb{S}}\leq 2^{-\frac{(N+1)}{p}},\qquad\mu\left(\left\|\mathbbm{1}_{A_{N}}F\right\|_{\mathbb{S}}>0\right)\leq 2^{-N}\|F\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}^{p},

hold, as well as

(3.25)

\lim_{N}\left\|\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus A_{-N-1}}F\right\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}=\lim_{N}\left\|\mathbbm{1}_{A_{N}}F\right\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}=0.

(3.26)

As a consequence, finite linear combination of
L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}
-atoms and
functions
F\in L^{\infty}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}
with
\mu\left(\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}>0\right)<\infty
are dense in
L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}
.

Proof.

Choose
\widetilde{A}_{k}\subset\mathbb{X}
such that

(3.27)

\left\|\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus\widetilde{A}^{k}}F\right\|_{\mathbb{S}}\leq 2^{\frac{k}{p}},\qquad\mu(\widetilde{A}_{k})\leq 2\mu\left(\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}>2^{\frac{k}{p}}\right).

Setting
A_{k}\coloneqq\bigcup_{l\geq k}\widetilde{A}_{l}
we guarantee that
A_{k}\subset A_{k-1}
and we have that

(3.28)

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu(A_{k})2^{k}\leq\begin{aligned} &\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{l\geq k}\mu(\widetilde{A}^{l})2^{k}\leq\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu(\widetilde{A}_{l})2^{l}\sum_{k\leq l}2^{k-l}\\
&\leq 2\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu(\widetilde{A}_{l})2^{l}\leq 4\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu\left(\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}>2^{\frac{l}{p}}\right)2^{l}\\
&\leq 4\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda\mu\left(\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}>\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda}\leq 4\|F\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}^{p}.\end{aligned}

Since
\Delta A_{k}=A_{k-1}\backslash A_{k}
, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
it holds
that

(3.29)

F=\sum_{k=-N}^{N}\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta A_{k}}F+\mathbbm{1}_{A_{N}}F+\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus A_{-N-1}}F.

To show the claims on outer Lebesgue norm convergence notice that

(3.30)

\Big\|\mathbbm{1}_{A_{N}}F\Big\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}^{p}\leq\int_{2^{N}}^{\infty}\lambda\mu(\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}>\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}})\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda}+2^{N}\mu(A_{N})

while

(3.31)

\Big\|\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{X}\setminus A_{-N-1}}F\Big\|_{L^{p}_{\mu}\mathbb{S}}^{p}\leq\int_{0}^{2^{-N-1}}\hskip-20.00003pt\lambda\mu(\|F\|_{\mathbb{S}}>\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}})\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{\lambda}.

∎

Proposition 3.3

(Restricted weak type interpolation for outer measure
spaces)
.

Let
(\mathbb{X}_{j},\mathbb{B}_{j},\mu_{j},\mathbb{S}_{j})
for each
j\in\{0,1,\ldots,M\}
be outer spaces and let

(3.32)

\Pi:\prod_{j=1}^{M}L^{\infty}_{\mu_{j}}\mathbb{S}_{j}\to\mathscr{R}\left(\mathbb{X}_{0}\right)\otimes^{k}{\Phi^{\infty}_{\mathfrak{r}}}^{\prime}

be an
M
-linear map. Let
\Omega_{\operatorname{restr}}\subset[0,1]^{M}
be the
set of points
(p_{1}^{-1},\ldots,p_{M}^{-1})\in[0,1]^{M}
such that the
restricted bounds

(3.33)

\Big\|\Pi(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M})\Big\|_{L^{p_{0},\infty}_{\mu_{0}}\mathbb{S}_{0}}\leq C_{\operatorname{restr}}(\vec{p})\prod_{j=1}^{M}\mu_{j}(\|F_{j}\|_{\mathbb{S}_{j}}>0)^{\frac{1}{p_{j}}}\|F_{j}\|_{\mathbb{S}_{j}}\qquad\forall F_{j}\in L^{\infty}_{\mu_{j}}\mathbb{S}_{j}

hold with
p_{0}^{-1}\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{M}p_{j}^{-1}
for some constant

C_{\operatorname{restr}}(\vec{p})>0
independent of
F_{j}
. Then

\Omega_{\operatorname{restr}}
is convex and if
(\overline{p}_{1}^{-1},\ldots,\overline{p}_{M}^{-1})\in[0,1]^{M}
is an interior point of

\Omega_{\operatorname{restr}}
then the strong bounds

(3.34)

\|\Pi(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M})\|_{L_{\mu_{0}}^{\overline{p}_{0}}\mathbb{S}_{0}}\leq C(\overrightarrow{\overline{p}})\prod_{j=1}^{M}\|F_{j}\|_{L_{\mu_{j}}^{\overline{p}_{j}}\mathbb{S}_{j}}\qquad\forall F_{j}\in L_{\mu_{j}}^{\overline{p}_{j}}\mathbb{S}_{j}

hold with
\overline{p}_{0}^{-1}\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{M}\overline{p}_{j}^{-1}
and some
C(\overrightarrow{\overline{p}})\geq 0
independent of
F_{j}
.

The proof of this fact is a direct adaptation of the proof of restricted weak
type interpolation for multilinear forms on classical Lebesgue spaces.

Proof.

Let us first show that
\Omega_{\operatorname{restr}}
is convex. Let
(p_{1,0}^{-1},\ldots,p_{M,0}^{-1}),(p_{1,1}^{-1},\ldots,p_{M,1}^{-1})\in\Omega_{\operatorname{restr}}
and let
\overline{p}_{j}^{-1}=(1-\theta)p_{j,0}^{-1}+\theta p_{j,1}^{-1}
for a fixed
\theta\in(0,1)
. By log
convexity of outer Lebesgue spaces ((
3.19
) in

Proposition

3.1
) it holds that

(3.35)

\displaystyle\Big\|\Pi(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M})\Big\|_{L_{\mu_{0}}^{\overline{p}_{0},\infty}\mathbb{S}_{0}}\hskip-20.00003pt\begin{aligned} &\hskip 20.00003pt\leq\Big\|\Pi(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M})\Big\|_{L_{\mu_{0}}^{\overline{p}_{0}}\mathbb{S}_{0}}\\
&\leq C_{\vec{p}_{0},\vec{p}_{1},\theta}\Big\|\Pi(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M})\Big\|_{L_{\mu_{0}}^{p_{0,0},\infty}\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{(1-\theta)}\Big\|\Pi(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M})\Big\|_{L_{\mu_{0}}^{p_{1,0},\infty}\mathbb{S}_{0}}^{\theta}\\
&\leq C_{\vec{p}_{0},\vec{p}_{1},\theta}C_{0}^{(1-\theta)}C_{1}^{\theta}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\mu_{j}(\|F_{j}\|_{\mathbb{S}_{j}}>0)^{\frac{1-\theta}{p_{j,0}}+\frac{\theta}{p_{j,1}}}\|F_{j}\|_{\mathbb{S}_{j}},\\
&\end{aligned}

\displaystyle C_{0}\coloneqq C_{\operatorname{restr}}((p_{1,0},\ldots,p_{M,0}))\qquad C_{1}\coloneqq C_{\operatorname{restr}}((p_{1,1},\ldots,p_{M,1})),

thus showing that
(\overline{p}_{1}^{-1},\ldots,\overline{p}_{M}^{-1})\in\Omega_{\operatorname{restr}}
, as required. We need to show that the bounds above
hold with the strong outer Lebesgue norms instead.

Assume that the factors on the right hand side of
(
3.34
) are finite and non-zero, for otherwise there
is nothing to prove. By homogeneity we may assume that
\|F_{j}\|_{L^{\overline{p}_{j}}_{\mu_{j}}\mathbb{S}_{j}}=1
for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
.
Using the atomic decomposition of
F_{j}
(proposition

3.2
) let us represent

(3.36)

F_{j}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}F_{j}^{k}\quad\text{with }F_{j}^{k}\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta A_{j}^{k}}F_{j}

and

(3.37)

\|F_{j}^{k}\|_{\mathbb{S}_{j}}\leq 2^{\frac{k}{\overline{p}_{j}}}\qquad\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mu_{j}(\Delta A^{k}_{j})2^{k}\approx 1.

We reason under the assumptions that the above sums are finite; the full
statement then follows by a limiting argument that we omit. We represent any

\vec{k}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{M})\in\mathbb{Z}^{M}
as

(3.38)

k_{i}=n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,i},

with
n\in\frac{1}{M}\mathbb{Z}
, for some
\vec{l}=(l_{1},\ldots,l_{M-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{M-1}
,
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{s}}=\left(\mathfrak{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{s}_{M-1}\right)\in\{-1,1\}^{M-1}
, and with
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}_{1},\ldots,\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}_{M-1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{M}_{0}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{M}
a pairwise orthogonal collection of vectors that we
denote by
\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}_{j}\coloneqq\left(\mathfrak{w}_{j,i}\right)_{i\in\{1,\ldots,M\}}
,
j\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}
, where

(3.39)

\mathbb{Z}^{M}_{0}\coloneqq\Big\{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{\vec{w}}}=(\mathfrak{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{w}_{M-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{M}:\sum_{j=1}^{M}\mathfrak{w}_{j}=0\Big\}.

This is always possible by choosing
n=M^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{M}k_{i}
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
large enough so that the map
\mathbb{Z}^{M-1}\ni(l_{1},\ldots,l_{M-1})\mapsto d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{w}}_{j}
is
surjective onto the set
M^{-1}{\mathbb{Z}^{M}_{0}}
. Using the above representation
of
\vec{k}
, we can decompose

(3.40)

\displaystyle\Pi(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M})={\sum_{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{s}}\in\{-1,1\}^{M-1}}}_{\mathfrak{\overrightarrow{}}}\Pi^{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{s}}}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M}),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Pi^{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{s}}}(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M})\coloneqq\\
&\qquad\sum_{n\in\frac{1}{M}\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{(l_{1},\ldots,l_{M-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{M}}\Pi\Big(F_{1}^{n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,1}},\ldots,F_{M}^{n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,M}}\Big).\end{aligned}

In the above notation we set
F_{i}^{k_{i}}=0
if
k_{i}\notin\mathbb{Z}
. Let us fix

\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{s}}
and show that (
3.34
) holds
for
\Pi^{\overrightarrow{\mathfrak{s}}}
in place of
\Pi
. There exists a point

\vec{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{M})\in(0,\infty)
with
(p_{1}^{-1},\ldots,p_{M}^{-1})
in the interior of
\Omega_{\operatorname{restr}}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{M}{p_{i}}^{-1}=\overline{p}_{0}^{-1}
and such that

(3.41)

\mathfrak{s}_{j}\sum_{i=1}^{M}(p_{i}^{-1}-\overline{p}_{i}^{-1})\mathfrak{w}_{j,i}>0\qquad\forall j\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}.

Let us choose an
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
small enough to have

(3.42)

\displaystyle((1\pm\varepsilon)^{-1}p_{1}^{-1},\ldots,(1\pm\varepsilon)^{-1}p_{M}^{-1})\in\Omega_{\operatorname{restr}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{j}\sum_{i=1}^{M}(p_{i}^{-1}-(1\pm\varepsilon)^{-1}\overline{p}_{i}^{-1})\mathfrak{w}_{j,i}>\varepsilon\qquad\forall j\in\{1,\ldots,M-1\}.

Using the restricted weak boundedness assumptions with the exponents above,
for any
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
it holds that

(3.43)

\displaystyle\mu_{0}\Big(\Big\|\Pi(F_{1}^{n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,1}},\ldots,F_{M}^{n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,M}})\Big\|_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}>\lambda^{\frac{1}{\overline{p}_{0}}}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{-(1\pm\varepsilon)}\prod_{i=1}^{M}\mu_{i}\Big(\|F_{i}\|_{\mathbb{S}_{i}}\Big)^{\frac{\overline{p}_{0}(1\pm\varepsilon)}{p_{i}(1\pm\varepsilon)}}2^{\frac{\overline{p}_{0}(1\pm\varepsilon)}{\overline{p}_{i}}\big(n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,i}\big)}

\displaystyle=\begin{aligned} &\lambda^{-(1\pm\varepsilon)}\prod_{i=1}^{M}\mu_{i}\Big(\Big\|F_{i}^{n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,i}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{S}_{i}}\Big)^{\frac{\overline{p}_{0}}{p_{i}}}2^{\frac{\overline{p}_{0}}{p_{i}}\big(n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,i}\big)}\\
&\qquad\times 2^{\pm\varepsilon n}2^{-\overline{p}_{0}d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathfrak{w}_{j,i}(p_{i}^{-1}-(1\pm\varepsilon)\overline{p}_{i}^{-1})}\end{aligned}

By the quasi-triangle inequality on
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}
, for a
sufficiently large
C>1
it holds that

(3.44)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mu_{0}\Big(\Big\|\sum_{n\in\frac{1}{M}\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{(l_{1},\ldots,l_{M-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{M}}\Pi(&F_{1}^{n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,1}},\ldots\Big.\Big.\\
&\qquad\Big.\Big.\ldots,F_{M}^{n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,M}})\Big\|_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}>C2^{\frac{n_{0}}{{\overline{p}_{0}}}}\Big)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\lesssim\begin{aligned} \sum_{n\in\frac{1}{M}\mathbb{Z}}&\sum_{(l_{1},\ldots,l_{M-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{M}}\mu_{0}\Big(\Big\|\Pi(F_{1}^{n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,1}},\ldots\Big.\Big.\\
&\qquad\Big.\Big.\ldots,F_{M}^{n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,M}})\Big\|_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}{>2^{\frac{n_{0}}{\overline{p}_{0}}-\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}}{\overline{p}_{0}}|n-n_{0}|-\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}}{\overline{p}_{0}}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}}}\Big)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\lesssim\begin{aligned} 2^{-n_{0}}&\sum_{(l_{1},\ldots,l_{M-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{M}}2^{-\overline{p}_{0}\varepsilon d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}}\sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$n\in\frac{1}{M}\mathbb{Z}$}}}2^{-(\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{\prime})|n-n_{0}|}\\
&\qquad\times\prod_{i=1}^{M}\mu_{i}\Big(\Big\|F_{i}^{n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,i}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{S}_{i}}\Big)^{\frac{\overline{p}_{0}}{p_{i}}}2^{\frac{\overline{p}_{0}}{p_{i}}\Big(n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,i}\Big)}.\end{aligned}

Summing over
n_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
we get that

(3.45)

\displaystyle\sum_{n_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}}\begin{aligned} \mu_{0}\Big(\Big\|\sum_{n\in\frac{1}{M}\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{(l_{1},\ldots,l_{M-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{M}}\Pi(&F_{1}^{n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,1}},\ldots\Big.\Big.\\
&\qquad\Big.\Big.\ldots,F_{M}^{n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,M}})\Big\|_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}>C2^{\frac{n_{0}}{{\overline{p}_{0}}}}\Big)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\lesssim\begin{aligned} \sum_{(l_{1},\ldots,l_{M-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{M}}2^{-\overline{p}_{0}\varepsilon d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}}\sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$n\in\frac{1}{M}\mathbb{Z}$}}}&\prod_{i=1}^{M}\mu_{i}\Big(\Big\|F_{i}^{n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,i}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{S}_{i}}\Big)^{\frac{\overline{p}_{0}}{p_{i}}}\\
&\qquad\times 2^{\frac{\overline{p}_{0}}{p_{i}}\Big(n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,i}\Big)}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\lesssim\begin{aligned} \sum_{(l_{1},\ldots,l_{M-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{M}}2^{-\overline{p}_{0}\varepsilon d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}}&\prod_{i=1}^{M}\Big(\sum_{n\in\frac{1}{M}\mathbb{Z}}\mu_{i}\Big(\Big\|F_{i}^{n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,i}}\Big\|_{\mathbb{S}_{i}}\Big)^{\overline{p}_{0}}\Big.\\
&\qquad\times\Big.2^{\overline{p}_{0}(n+d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}\mathfrak{s}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{j,i})}\Big)^{\frac{p_{i}}{\overline{p}_{0}}}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{(l_{1},\ldots,l_{M-1})\in\mathbb{N}^{M}}2^{-\overline{p}_{0}\varepsilon d^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{M-1}l_{j}}\lesssim 1

∎

As a result we deduce the outer Hölder inequality used extensively in the
context of outer Lebesgue spaces; variants include
[
AU20a
, Proposition
2.34]
,
[
DT15
, Proposition
3.4]
.

Corollary 3.4

(Hölder inequality for outer Lebesgue spaces)
.

For each

j\in\{0,1,\ldots,M\}
let

(\mathbb{X}_{j},\mathbb{B}_{j}^{\cup},\mu_{j},\mathbb{S}_{j})
be an outer space. Let

(3.46)

\Pi:\prod_{j=1}^{M}L^{\infty}_{\mu_{j}}\mathbb{S}_{j}\to L^{\infty}_{\mu_{0}}\mathbb{S}_{j}

be an
M
-linear map such that

(3.47)

\|\Pi(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M})\|_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}\leq C_{\infty}\prod_{j=1}^{M}\|F_{j}\|_{\mathbb{S}_{j}}

and suppose that for all
F_{j}\in L^{\infty}_{\mu_{j}}\mathbb{S}_{j}
it holds that

(3.48)

\mu_{0}(\|\Pi(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M})\|_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}>0)\leq\min_{j=1,\ldots,M}\mu_{j}\bigl(\|F_{j}\|_{\mathbb{S}_{j}}>0\bigr).

Then for all
\vec{p}=(p_{1},\ldots,p_{M})\in(0,\infty]^{M}
it holds that

(3.49)

\|\Pi(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M})\|_{L_{\mu_{0}}^{p_{0}}\mathbb{S}_{0}}\lesssim_{p_{j}}C\prod_{j=1}^{M}\|F_{j}\|_{L_{\mu_{j}}^{p_{j}}\mathbb{S}_{j}}

with
p_{0}^{-1}\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{M}p_{j}^{-1}
.

Proof.

The claim above follows by noticing that

(3.50)

\|\Pi(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M})\|_{L_{\mu_{0}}^{p_{0}}\mathbb{S}_{0}}\begin{aligned} &\leq\mu_{0}\left(\|\Pi(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M})\|_{\mathbb{S}_{0}}>0\right)^{\frac{1}{p_{0}}}\|\Pi(F_{1},\ldots,F_{M})\|_{L_{\mu_{0}}^{\infty}\mathbb{S}_{0}}\\
&\leq\prod_{j=1}^{M}\mu_{j}(\|F_{j}\|_{\mathbb{S}_{j}}>0)^{\frac{1}{p_{j}}}\|F_{j}\|_{\mathbb{S}_{j}}\end{aligned}

and applying
Proposition

3.3
.
∎

The next statement is usually referred to as outer Radon-Nikodym. It has been
used extensively in results proved using the framework of outer Lebesgue
spaces e.g. in
[
AU20a
, Proposition 2.33]
,

[
AU20
, Corollary 4.13]
,

[
Ura16
, Lemma 2.2]
. We merely
sketch its proof. This result allows one to compare classical integrals with

L^{1}
-type outer Lebesgue quasi-norms e.g. the one appearing on

\mathrm{LHS}{\eqref{eq:RN-domination-BHT}}
, that represents the dual form to

\mathrm{BHT}_{\beta}
in terms of embedded functions.

Proposition 3.5

(Radon-Nikodym -type domination)
.

Let
(\mathbb{X},\mathbb{B}^{\cup},\mu,\mathbb{S})
be an outer space and
let
\mathfrak{m}
be a Radon measure on
\mathbb{X}
and let
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{k}\in\Phi_{\mathfrak{r}}^{\infty}
